22/01/2018, 12:11 - Akhil Coach: This is one of the best answer which i read because it explains the science behind the most important thing which makes our life good : (HAPPINESS)

Question was :

I am rich, fit, and I have mastered almost everything I wanted to master. Why am I still not happy and still not satisfied?

It starts like this

“Human happiness (as we know it) is caused by 4 basic chemicals:

Dopamine

Endorphins

Serotonin

Oxytocin

On your journey to become fit, your body released endorphins to cope with the pain of physical exercise.

You probably began to enjoy exercise as you got more into it, and the endorphins made you happy - temporarily.


On your journey to become rich, you probably completed many tasks and goals.

You probably bought all the things you’ve ever wanted. Nice cars, beautiful clothes, and a perfect home.

This released dopamine in your brain when you achieved your goals and bought these things, which once again contributed to your happiness - temporarily.


So what about the other two chemicals?

It turns out that human happiness is incomplete without all 4 chemicals constantly being released in the brain.

So now you need to work on releasing serotonin and oxytocin.

“How do I do that, Karim?”

Serotonin is released when we act in a way that benefits others. When we give to causes beyond ourselves and our own benefit. When we connect with people on a deep, human level.

Writing this Quora answer is releasing serotonin in my brain right now because I’m using my precious time on the weekend to give back to others for free.

Hopefully I’m providing useful information that can help other people, like yourself.

That’s why you often see billionaires turning to charity when they have already bought everything they wanted to, and experienced everything they wanted to in life.

They’ve had enough dopamine from material pleasures, now they need the serotonin.



Oxytocin on the other hand, is released when we become close to another human being.

When we hug a friend,or shake someone’s hand, oxytocin is released in varying amounts.

Oxytocin is easy to release. It’s all about becoming more social!

Share your wealth with your friends and family to create amazing experiences.

Laugh, love, cooperate, and play with others.



I think it all comes down to the likelihood that you are missing two things: contribution and social connection”

Send this instead !

29/01/2018, 12:42 - +91 99455 94461: There was a Jewish man named Yankel, who had a bakery, in a town, Crown Heights, Germany. He always said, "You know why I’m alive today?"

He said "I was a kid, just a teenager at the time in Germany.

Nazis were killing Jews with no mercy.

We were on the train being taken to Auschwitz by Nazis. Night came and it was deathly cold in that compartment.

The Germans left us on the side of the tracks overnight, sometimes for days, without any food. There were no blankets to keep us warm. Snow was falling everywhere. Cold winds were hitting our cheeks, every second. We were hundreds of people in that terribly cold night. No food. No water. No shelter. No blankets ".

"The blood in our bodies started freezing. It was becoming ice.

Beside me, there was a beloved elderly Jewish man from my hometown. He was shivering from head to toe, and looked

terrible. So I wrapped my arms around him to warm him up.

I hugged him tightly to give him some heat. I rubbed his arms, his legs, his face, his neck. I begged him to try to be alive.

I encouraged him.

All night long, I kept this man warm this way.

I was tired, and freezing cold myself. My fingers were numb, but I didn’t stop rubbing heat into that old man’s body.

Hours and hours went by. Finally, morning came and the sun began

to shine. I looked around to see the other people.

To my horror, all I could see were frozen bodies. All I could hear was deathly silence.

Nobody else in that cabin were alive. That freezing night killed them all.

They died from the cold. Only two people survived: the old man and me.

The old man survived because I kept him warm... and I survived because I was warming him.

May I tell you the secret to survival in this world?

When you warm other people’s hearts, you will remain warm yourself.

When you support, encourage and inspire others, then you will discover support, encouragement and inspiration in your own life as well.

My friends, That is the secret to a happy life.

- Artificial intelligence is no match for natural stupidity.

20/02/2018, 10:09 - Akhil Coach: Zen Story:  

"Zen masters give personal guidance in a secluded room. No one enters while teacher and pupil are together.

Mokurai, the Zen master of Kennin temple in Kyoto, used to enjoy talking with merchants and newspapermen as well as with his pupils. A certain tubmaker was almost illiterate. He would ask foolish questions of Mokurai, have tea, and then go away.

One day while the tubmaker was there Mokurai wished to give personal guidance to a disciple, so he asked the tubmaker to wait in another room.

“I understand you are a living Buddha,” the man protested. “Even the stone Buddhas in the temple never refuse the numerous persons who come together before them. Why then should I be excluded?”

Mokurai had to go outside to see his disciple."

20/12/2021, 07:44 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

The general buzz over the past 2-3 weeks has been around the appointment of two Indian-origin CEOs for global organizations - Parag Agrawal for Twitter and Leena Nair for Chanel.

But beyond their Indian origins and other firsts, I thought there were  interesting inferences.

Twitter is truly a Tech Platform company at its core and promoted their CTO to the role of CEO (somewhat rare in business circles) - perhaps an indication of the value of Tech and therefore someone best positioned to capitalise the power of Tech and monetize the same.

On the other side was Chanel, a company with rich legacy in fashion and truly dependent on people - _employees_ to take pride in building differentiated experience and building advocates of their _customers_ . Maybe that led to one of the best global CHROs to be anointed as the CEO (again somewhat rare).

It perhaps speaks to the clarity of *purpose* of these organizations and picking up the person most aligned to the same thru expertise, experience and culture.

Have a great purposeful week ahead ⛅☀️🙏.

26/01/2022, 21:19 - Sanjay Gupta: Watching bit of Australian Open... watches great match between Medvedev and Alisiamme and it inspired me to write a bit.. sharing with you. Respond what you think..
26/01/2022, 21:20 - Sanjay Gupta: In Australian open history, this would go down as one of the best matches ever played. Medvedev's comeback from 2 sets and a match point down to win in 5 sets. He defeated Auger-Aliassime 6-7, 3-6, 7-6, 7-5, 6-4 in more than 4 hrs of play.

Jim Courier interviewed Medvedev after QF match in Australian Open, asks him "How did you come back from 2 sets down and match point down". Guess what is Medvedev's first response  - "I don't know" and then he started describing what he thinks happened. Key insight from this conversation is that - you cannot copy what successful people describe as what made them successful, because they cannot describe it. 'How to'  do not work. There are only principles but not prescription. You have to discover your own methods, your own how to. 

Another insight from the interview was; when Medvedev responded to question about "how he turned it around, what was he saying to himself", he said that "I asked myself what Novak would do in this situation". Think about why a player ranked 2nd in the world, grand slam champion, favorite to with this grand slam is confused and thinking about how other champion would respond in this situation. So what is the key insight from scenario, is that even champions can get confused and reframing the problem can give you different perspectives/insights that you can work with.

29/01/2022, 07:19 - Sanjay Gupta: Sports coaching is a growing field, almost all professional players and teams have coaches that help with the skills and mental side of coaching. Coaching is now integral to sports. But there is one sport that is an exception, or at least a partial exception. Almost all tennis players have coaches but coaching is not allowed in Tennis, surprised, but it is true. Tennis is one of few sports that does not allow in-match coaching. Tennis has been stuck in tradition and reluctant to change, probably it is part of the appeal, as it is seen as an individual sport and battle is between the physical and mental side of two players, and any external assistance would contaminate the purity of that battle. 

But when the stakes are very high (like in Grand Slam matches), there are violations of these rules. The recent controversy happened in the Australian Open Semifinal between Tsitsipas and Medvedev. Tsistipas coach is his father, in the heat of the match he shouted some instructions and got a coaching violation warning this proved counterproductive and demotivated Tsistipas and he lost the 4th set tamely and the match.

Now the question is, Should Tennis change with time and allow coaching during play? Or should Tennis remain traditional and keep the purity of battle between two individual athletes and not bring coaches during the play?

What do you think about this as professional coaches?

I had an interesting experience during the week, spending time @ the hospital - not for myself, and nothing critical. The nurses left an indelible impact through their service orientation. However, as with the Formula 1 team, the dance required many parts beyond the dancer to come together to create the impact - the music, the visual impact, and others. Not very different from what we see in business. There is a need for a leader, but without a high-performing team, results cannot be delivered. And the most critical part of the ecosystem is the "customer" - without customers, there is no business. 

Have a great week ahead ⛅☀️🙏.

24/10/2022, 08:48 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

In the past week, had an opportunity to experience true "customer-centricity". I had contacted a mobile XRay provider to get X-ray done for my father.

If you did not know, this service exists and useful for people with mobility constraints. The technology is amazing and generates the images on the laptop immediately after the XRay that can be shared within 15 minutes. I was amazed with the technology.

Yet what impressed me more was the service provider - his responsiveness and his empathy. He was courteous when I called him, came home exactly at the time that i had requested, provided his services under constrained environment with patience and diligence, and provided me the information i required within 15 minutes.

I realised that Technology is a great enabler and yet it requires an empathetic and responsive human using the Tech to bring in "differentiated customer experience".

As we celebrate Diwali, may the festival of lights bring in additional light and visibility to your path ahead.

Happy Diwali and have a great week ahead ⛅☀️🙏.

12/12/2022, 07:55 - Sanjay Gupta: @⁨Anant Krishnan⁩ great insights. 
This inspired me to write about one event of the world cup and some insights I got. Sharing below.
12/12/2022, 07:55 - Sanjay Gupta: You deleted this message
12/12/2022, 07:56 - Sanjay Gupta: Events happen, do they define us, should they define us?

Harry Kane is the captain of English football team.
Harry Kane is the leader of men, the team loves and respects Harry Kane. 
Harry Kane has conducted himself with grace and dignity in the pitch, in the dressing room (probably the most important place that people do now know about) and off the pitch.
Harry Kane has taken 69 penalties for England. He had scored 58 of them, missed 11 of these.
In the World Cup Quarter Final Match against Arch Rival France, he scored in the first penalty. 

And then the event happened.  
Harry Kane missed the 2nd penalty and England lost the match.

How might he be seen from now on?
For the most people, what happened before that dreaded penalty is forgotten, and what would be remembered forever is that, Harry Kane, the captain of the English team, missed the most important penalty of his life. It would define his career, his legacy, him. Fair or unfair, you judge.

What's more important?
More than how others define Harry Kane, It's how he looks at that one event of his life, is important. 
That one event is just an event out of thousands other events that happened in his life. And it becomes important not at that time it's happening, but what meaning we give to it later, what stories we tell about it later.  

Is there any learning for us?
Events do not define us, what label we give to it, what story we tell to ourselves, what meaning we attach to it define us.
How often we put high importance to some past event in our life, especially negative meaning, and that keeps dragging us in our present. 

What can help us?
Detached attachment. Perspective. Sambhav. Faith in higher being. Reframing. 
These and many others are some ways to see the events as just that, events. We learn from it and move on. 
Events do not define us. Events must not define us.

16/01/2023, 09:15 - Sanjay Gupta: As always great connect to current and deep insights. @⁨Anant Krishnan⁩ 


IMHO

I think the key principles in play here is "Incentives drive behavior"
We are creatures of habits. The rewards drive actions. 

Investors want to maximize returns and minimize risks. And they have found "Cricket" as that cash cow. It's now the flywheel that is  spinning and more and more is being invested to keep the momentum.

Hockey is not that yet. And probably could not match "cricket".

And that's the reality to be accepted and then market and promote in alignment with cricket schedule and not in conflict.

You cannot ignore 800 pound guerilla in the room, acknowledge it and make it your friend.

In other fields - for individual decision maker it's again about maximizing upside and minimizing downside.
For anything to change - it's policy makers who would need to artificially increase incentives for sometime for anything new to take roots. E.g. like in electric cars that are not profitable in the beginning, need government incentives to come to level where they can now compete with ICE vehicles.

13/02/2023, 07:52 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning.
Hope you all had a good week.

Many of us would have heard or participated in discussions around purpose-led organizations and leaders.

Had an opportunity to observe this in a facility for dementia patients. 
The founder founded this institution 7 years back - he had an engineering background and no medical background. However, his close family member had dementia, and he had to take care of her. This created a deep sense of purpose. When you talk to him, you sense his deep  compassion and empathy for dementia patients.

He has built a team that strongly identifies with his purpose - be it his operations lead or the other staff, who interface with patients - submitting themselves to this cause of making life better for the patients.

They do not seem to do any chest-thumping - perhaps their actions alone provide them the fulfillment, that drives them.

Provides for inspiration and learning....

06/03/2023, 06:25 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

During the week, I had the opportunity to become aware of the term "neuro-diversity" through an expert in this field.

The pareto principle of 80-20 applies here too, where 80% of the world is "neuro-typical" (what the world considers normal) and 20% is "neuro-divergent" (world is perhaps unforgiving and considers them abnormal).

The message that got me to think was that our ecosystem (education, career progression, ..) are all designed for the former and rarely considers the latter - our collective loss.
Something for all of us to ponder.

10/07/2023, 09:58 - Sanjay Gupta: Thanks @⁨Anant Krishnan⁩ for sharing this.
And this made me think a bit more about "imposter syndrome". 
I would share my personal opinion/experience some other time. 
A quick research (quotes) on what it is.

"The imposter syndrome phenomenon is an internal experience of believing that you are not as competent as others perceive you to be. In many ways, it is a form of self-sabotage." - Valerie Young, author of The Imposter Syndrome: Overcome the Secret Fear That Holds You Back

"The biggest challenge with imposter syndrome is the fear of being found out. But the truth is, everyone feels like an imposter sometimes. The important thing is to remember that you are not alone." - Amy Cuddy, social psychologist and author of Presence: Bringing Your Boldest Self to Life

"Imposter syndrome is not a sign of weakness. It is a sign that you are pushing yourself outside of your comfort zone and challenging yourself to grow." - Brené Brown, author of Daring Greatly: How the Courage to Be Vulnerable Transforms the Way We Live, Love, Parent, and Lead

Thanks

17/07/2023, 09:22 - Sanjay Gupta: Sports are not just entertaining; they also teach important life lessons in a direct and genuine way.

In the recent Wimbledon finals, both favorites lost, but for different reasons. Ons Jabeur lost because she wanted to win so badly and was afraid of losing, which made her tense. This shows that one person relied on external validation (winning) to feel good and felt pressure, while the other person believed in themselves and focused on the process and being in the moment.

It's not easy to have this mindset, but it's an important lesson to learn.

24/07/2023, 09:21 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

One of the sports highlights has been the winning of the Korean Open badminton doubles title by Chirag Shetty and Satwik Rankireddy.

They have been playing together for over 6 years. The pair was posing questions and challenging established pairs, yet titles came slow. In 2022-2023 they have gone to an elevated space and being more consistent , have already won 4 titles within a short period.

The challenge is to sustain the pairing and continue to trust each other. We have had a similar case of a pair in tennis - Leander and Bhupathi - who split, when they were at the top. Their relationship went South pretty quick and they could not even see each other,  let alone pair up with each other.

What makes a pair click ? Of course, capability is paramount, yet not sufficient. The trust and respect they carry for each other makes the ultimate difference.

In businesses too, we pair or perhaps have a core team of 3-4. This is a team of peers, and therefore, there is the risk of one-upmanship and therefore loss of trust.

What had been your secret sauce ?

Have a great trustworthy week ahead ⛅☀️🙏.
25/07/2023, 06:37 - Sanjay Gupta: I have seen Chirag and Satwik play. They were always super high energy, passionate, expressive, and aggressive. What I doubted was that can they sustain this for long years and match this against Koreans, Chinese, Malaysians, And European players. They have proved everyone wrong. 

About what makes a pair click?

One mental model to look from - Structure (external) and Individual (Ego internal). 
In Tennis, doubles do not command the same respect as singles. Badminton it is respected as much as singles.
So every tennis player wants to be recognized first as a singles player. To click as pair, they have to suppress this desire for the greater good (pair success).  Best doubles pair in tennis have been where it was easier for them to do. Many best pairs were brothers/sisters (Bryan, Woodbridge, Williams) or one player is a far superior singles player than the other (McEnroe and Fleming, Navratilova and Pamshiver). It was easier to in this pair without ego clashes. 
Badminton - you are a team from the beginning, you are recognized at the same level as singles so you do not desire (that much) to go individual way. 

The same analysis can be reframed as an incentive, What's the incentive to move out or remain together.

Thanks

31/07/2023, 08:38 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

I was somewhat intrigued by the idea of "average" statistically speaking, and how this may be misleading.

This came to life through the rainfall that India has had in 2023. The "average" rainfall across India has been reported to be within normal range - something we could derive joy from.
But wait - the "normal" range is due to the excessive rainfall access North and other regions, while having severe deficits across parts of Southern region. The farmers at both ends are adversely affected by this, leading to an adverse impact on their living, inspite of the average being in the normal range.

That's why they say "devil is in the details".

Perhaps not too different from business teams delivering outcomes. Some team members have to do the heavy lifting, which could even impact their health. It could be a function of the roles, geographies, industry segments and other factors that could contribute to a skewed performance by certain individuals and yet the average looks fine.

Your perspective ?

Have a great week ahead ⛅☀️🙏.

Other principle is from SAFe - synchronization and cadence. This is about brining this principle of sustainable pace and collective optimization to life. 

Sacrifice for collective good - whatever that collective is, is the mindset.

14/08/2023, 07:48 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

Recently I required some specialised service, and therefore approached the designated specialist service center. The person in there did not inspire confidence both in terms of what needed done as well as his willingness. 

Not having too many options, approached a generalist with not a lot of confidence, since he may not even know what is involved. As I explained to him, he immediately demonstrated a keen understanding, and his tone conveyed his willingness and commitment. My sceptical self, was pleasantly surprised when he delivered the task to our satisfaction, and that too at a nominal fee. He clearly demonstrated the will firstly, adapting his core skills, thereby scoring high on satisfaction. I have become a huge advocate for this person.

Am sure, we have faced these scenarios in our business lives as well - feeling let down by a designated specialist, but completely surprised by someone whom we rarely expected to scale up,  taking up the challenge and delivering the same.

Point to ponder : 
Should we provide the opportunity to someone that demonstrates the willingness ? Should we take up opportunities ourselves when we may not have the complete skillsets ?

Have a wonderful week ahead ⛅☀️🙏.

02/10/2023, 09:02 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you had a good week.

Today is Gandhi Jayanti. It's an opportunity to reflect on what's happening around us from time to time.

We had two days of city and state shutdown - Bandhs - within a week.

The cause is absolutely valid - which is to safe guard the water availability of our state, especially when the erratic rain has caused a water shortfall.

The question is about the "means" to address the "cause" to get to the "desired outcome".

Assuming the "intent" of the Bandh is to address this cause, the "impact"  is different on different strata of the society. 
For many segments of our society, it's inconvenience or financial losses at worst, yet we are fortunate not to let this incapacitate us. 

The individuals in the unorganised sector are impacted the worst - they have been stolen of their daily living - and could incapacitate them substantially.

This is perhaps a good time to see how to adapt Gandhian means to address causes that need addressed.

Happy Gandhi Jayanti.

06/11/2023, 08:54 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

Cricket is a religion in India, and perhaps the one that unifies the entire nation. We float in a euphoria when things go well. We are equally and more unforgiving as arm chair experts when things don't go well. 

As much as many players have been contributing to the collective success of the team in the current World Cup, one person that has stood out is Virat Kohli. We are all gaga over his performance.

Rewind the tape to the period 2020-2022. Virat was but a pale reflection of his extraordinary self, and looked a mere mortal. Most of the nation in its religious fervour asked many uncomfortable questions about him, suggesting that his "shelf life" may have been over. 
He took a break. He came back from the break and slowly eased into respectable performances in 2022 and has come into his own in 2023.

Wish we could unravel what he went thru during his lean patch, how he motivated himself during this period, and how he bounced back. 

This is what leaders are made of -  be it sports or business. 
They are super-focused, resilient, always on a quest to be a better version of themselves, and motivated by the opportunity to contribute to a larger purpose. 

Such leaders inspire us.
Have we inspired others ?

The festivities in India typically start in the second half of the year with us reaching the crescendo by the end of the year.

This month we celebrate  Deepawali / Diwali - the festival of Deepas / Diyas, the festival of lights. It signifies the victory of light over darkness.

This celebration is important to reaffirm our confidence in self.

19/01/2024, 09:01 - Akhil Coach: Here is my response to my client: Thank you very much, XXXi, for creating such a metaphor for our relationship. It serves as a testament to your supportive guidance, as long as it continues to be beneficial to you. Your words convey a sense of deep reverence and appreciation, acknowledging someone who, though initially unassuming, reveals a significant depth of virtues upon closer acquaintance.

Bearing this in mind, I encourage my fellow coaches to contemplate our roles with our clients. It is vital to recognize moments when our identity or ego might be at risk and to consider the possibility of becoming co-explorers rather than positioning ourselves as gurus, experts, sages, or fixers. Inviting clients into a space of co-exploration can foster a more generative environment. This is much more effective than a stance of 'let me fix you' or 'let me show you how intelligent I am,' which clients often do not have the time for and indeed do not need.

Indeed, as in the case with xxxi, there may be times when a client develops a positive transference that they can harness for their own growth. As coaches, we should partake in this process without giving in to our egos or solidified identities. Our role should be clear: to facilitate, rather than to dictate or assume a superior stance of 'I’m OK and you’re not OK,' particularly if one assumes the role of a guru. Our aim is to support the journey of our clients' self-discovery and development. <This message was edited>

There is so much competition in that space as with other areas, and they have to work hard to "stay relevant", maintain followership, and earn thru their performances.

For me, the larger point is that anything as a hobby or additional interest could be a de-stressor, while the same as a profession could create stress over a period of time.

As he concludes, as is a good advice for all of us, when the stress exceeds the acceptable threshold (different for each of us) and not getting mitigated with levers within our control, do not hesitate to seek professional help.

Your thoughts?

Have a great de-stressed week ahead ⛅☀️🙏.

10/10/2024, 19:37 - Akhil Coach: Ratan Tata is one of India’s most influential industrialists and philanthropists, born on 28th December 1937. His words and ideas have inspired countless entrepreneurs to pursue their dreams and build successful businesses. A true role model for the youth, not only in India but across the world, Ratan Tata’s legacy is marked by visionary leadership, humility, and a deep commitment to social good.

Here are 20 of the best quotes by Ratan Tata that inspire success and personal growth:

	1.	“I don’t believe in taking the right decisions. I take decisions and then make them right.”
	2.	“Ups and downs in life are very important to keep us going, because a straight line, even in an ECG, means we are not alive.”
	3.	“None can destroy iron, but its own rust can. Likewise, none can destroy a person but their own mindset.”
	4.	“There are many things that, if I had to relive, I might do differently. But I would not like to look back and dwell on what I haven’t done.”
	5.	“If you want to walk fast, walk alone. But if you want to walk far, walk together.”
	6.	“Take the stones people throw at you, and use them to build a monument.”
	7.	“Power and wealth are not two of my main stakes.”
	8.	“I may have hurt some people along the way, but I would like to be remembered as someone who did their best to do the right thing and never compromised on integrity.”
	9.	“The day I am not able to fly will be a sad day for me.”
	10.	“A person who tries to copy others may succeed for a while, but true long-term success comes from originality.”
	11.	“Don’t be too serious. Enjoy life as it comes.”
	12.	“I admire people who are successful, but if that success comes with too much ruthlessness, I can’t respect it.”
	13.	“Businesses need to go beyond their interests and serve the communities they operate in.”
	14.	“I don’t believe in work-life balance. I believe in work-life integration. Make your work and life meaningful and fulfilling, and they will complement each other.”
	15.	“Leadership is about taking responsibility, not making excuses.”
	16.	“Don’t wait for opportunities to come to you—create your own opportunities.”
	17.	“One day, you’ll realize that material things mean nothing. What truly matters is the well-being of the people you love.”
	18.	“The best leaders are those who surround themselves with people smarter than they are.”
	19.	“Success and ethics should go hand in hand. Business success should never come at the cost of integrity.”
	20.	“Never underestimate the power of kindness, empathy, and compassion in your interactions with others.”

These timeless quotes capture Ratan Tata’s wisdom and his approach to leadership, business, and life.

रावण में कई अच्छाइयाँ थीं, जो उसे एक अद्वितीय व्यक्तित्व बनाती हैं। वह महापंडित थे—चारों वेदों के ज्ञाता, और शिव के परम भक्त थे। उन्होंने *शिव तांडव स्तोत्र* की रचना की थी। रावण एक कुशल राजनीतिज्ञ भी थे, और उन्होंने राम की सेना को पराजित करने के लिए उत्कृष्ट रणनीतियाँ बनाई थीं। इसके अलावा, रावण का योगदान *तंत्र विद्या और विज्ञान* में भी उल्लेखनीय था, और उन्हें एक प्रकांड वैज्ञानिक के रूप में जाना जाता था, जिन्होंने *इंद्रजाल* जैसी विद्या का अनुसंधान किया था।

लंका का वैभव रावण के शासनकाल में अपने चरम पर था, जिसके कारण उनकी नगरी को 'सोने की लंका' कहा जाता था। वह आयुर्वेद और तंत्र का मर्मज्ञ थे और *नाड़ी परीक्षा* जैसी चिकित्सा पद्धतियों का विकास किया था। उनकी राजनीतिक कुशलता के साथ-साथ, रावण एक अच्छा शासक भी थे, जिन्होंने अपने राज्य में शांति और समृद्धि बनाए रखी।

*_रावण में नैतिकता भी थी। जब उन्होंने सीता का हरण किया, तो वह शास्त्रों की मर्यादा का पालन करते हुए उन्हें स्पर्श तक नहीं किया। यह एक महत्वपूर्ण गुण था, जो उन्हें दूसरों से अलग करता है।_* 

हालांकि, इन सभी गुणों के बावजूद, रावण के अवगुण, जैसे कि अहंकार, ईर्ष्या और प्रतिशोध, उनके सभी सद्गुणों पर भारी पड़े। वाल्मीकि रामायण में भी उनके बारे में यही कहा गया है कि उनके अहंकार और अधर्म ने उन्हें बर्बाद कर दिया। यही कारण है कि वह आज बुराई का प्रतीक माने जाते हैं। उनके इन अवगुणों पर भगवद्गीता के अध्याय 16, श्लोक 4 का श्लोक सटीक रूप से लागू होता है:

*”दम्भो दर्पोऽभिमानश्च क्रोधः पारुष्यमेव च।*
*अज्ञानं चाभिजातस्य पार्थ सम्पदामासुरीम्॥”*
(भगवद्गीता 16.4)

(अर्थ: पाखंड, घमंड, अभिमान, क्रोध, कठोरता और अज्ञानता—ये सभी आसुरी गुण हैं।)

रावण के इस पतन से हमें यह सिखने को मिलता है कि केवल ज्ञान और शक्ति किसी को महान नहीं बनाते। अच्छा बनने के लिए नैतिकता और मर्यादा का होना आवश्यक है। इसी कारण रावण, जो सभी गुणों से सम्पन्न था, एक त्रासदी का पात्र बन गया।

इस विजयदशमी पर, आइए हम यह संकल्प लें कि हम अपने भीतर के अवगुणों को पहचानें और उन्हें त्यागने का हर संभव प्रयास करें, ताकि हमारे जीवन में अच्छाई और सद्गुण ही सदा विजयी रहें।

21/10/2024, 08:04 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

The curtains are coming down on another stellar sports career - that of one of my favorite sports personas, Rafael Nadal. He has less than a month for his final swan song, as he bids goodbye to the sport that enabled him to win the hearts of many.

Nadal burst on the international scene as a teenager challenging the infallible Federer (he is another of my favorites 😎). 

The contrast is so starc - Federer is the ever-graceful and elegant like the premium Swiss watch, while  Nadal is the epitome of the never-say-die attitude like the Spanish matador no one could ever take lightly. Their relationship is what legends are made of - from being fierce competitors, to immense respect for each other while not losing their competitive streak on the court,  finally graduating into  camaraderie and deep friendship. 
I would be remiss if I do not highlight the contribution of the third of the triumvirate - Djokovic -  in adding to the  competitiveness of tennis over the past decade. 

I admired Nadal for his work ethic, passion and love for the game, never-say-die attitude, resilience, authenticity and respect for his opponents. 

Now thats a lot to ask of any human being, but leaders are made of a different fabric. They bring in their authentic style by "being" and "doing" consistently and on a sustained basis - something for us to ponder.

28/10/2024, 11:10 - Prabhaker: *About India's series loss:* Leaders should address symptoms early and decisively,  and have the foresight delve deep into incidents too minor to notice. I recall an example where a lot of flights in a war were returning with hits to some parts of the flight and they said, lets make these part strong. But someone correctly identified that flights that were NOT  returning were hit at other part of the flight and never returned! And those were in fact weak parts to be addressed!! Similarly, cracks in India's batting were visible way back when playing with Srilanka. The one day series loss there proved that Indian batsmens' legendary skill against spin was crumbling. Rohit Sharma at that time remarked 'we should have played more horizontal shots' (ironically, repeating something similar now after loss with NZ).  There was another warning sign as well: rookies like Ashwin and Pant were making centuries while legendary batsmen like Rohit and Kohli were not getting to terms with the pitch. Team leadership had many months to fix these things, including a toy series with Bangladesh.  Leadership is all about facing the truth, even if it hurts. Instead I think they were in 'denial' -- afterall India is best when it comes to spin! Hopefully,  Indian Leadership comes out of the denial and fixes the things needed to be fixed. If not, -- they will be foxed in Australia (this time by their spinners, and not pacemen!).

Everyone talks about  creating customer delight through differentiated experience.
Here is a live story that transpired last week.

In India, we are entering a peak wedding season, and needless to say, there is a lot of demand for venues that host the weddings. Some of them take an end-to-end responsibility for the conduct of the weddings including the catering. Food plays an important part in making the weddings memorable.

The occasion was food tasting -  an exhaustive and exclusive arrangement of food items, from which each family could select their preferences for their wedding. Creating experience seems to be about evoking  emotions of joy and awe in the targeted customers.

11/11/2024, 07:15 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning.  Hope you had a good week.

One of the news items that kept many of us engaged was the US elections - all the projections pre-election, the excitement of the election results coming in thru the day culminating in a Trump triumph, and the post election debates and narratives. 

One thing that sounded interesting to me is that Trump as a persona seems to represent many things that are counter to the typical  characterization of a leader in today's context.

However what may have resonated about him to his constituents are his 
1. authenticity - very comfortable within the skin of himself, 
2. courage of conviction - not worried about others' perceptions and perspectives, and 
3. decisiveness. 
The followers wanted a leader that would act on his commitments and take tough decisions, under the current circumstances. 

It reaffirmed the fact that different situations would demand different leadership styles. Typically,   a war-time general would have very different mindsets and skillsets than a peace-time general. Can the same leader fit into both roles- some could, while others may find it challenging. 

Point to ponder - what kind of a leader are you and what situations do you typically excel in.

India is truly a land of diversity and rich cultural inheritage, as I got to experience some of it over the weekend.

On one side, India has the large cities with it's skyscrapers, and the corporate world embedded in them, similar to any city across the globe.

25/11/2024, 09:15 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

IPL players auction is a mega event spread over 2 days. Needless to say, day 1 - yesterday,  provided a reasonable trajectory of each team's strategy . It also provided for surprises.

As I watched the event,  couple of interesting points came to fore.

1. Teams with clarity of strategy went after specific players and tried to get them into their teams, even if it warranted a high premium. On the other hand, some teams went after desirable players but did not see them through, losing those players out to other teams.
This is not dissimilar to organizations with clear vision and strategy willing to invest in multiple areas including paying premiums to acquire talent to execute to their strategies.
2. The amount for which the players gets auctioned for, need not necessarily point to their intrinsic value. It's a function of perceived value based on market demand.
This is not very different from the valuations of organizations - it's typically based on future potential and promise. 
We also get to observe this in remunerations offered across different professions - Computer Science graduates working on technology being valued far higher than medical professionals working with humans, during their formative years.

Curious to know your perspectives.

Have a wonderful week ahead 🌤️☀️🙏.

Last week we lost a musician extraordinaire and an exceptional human being - Ustad Zakir Hussain. He was uncomfortable being addressed as Ustad - a term that elevates an individual to a revered status. He had earned the respect of  "creme de la creme" of musicians across the world, as much as any  normal music-discerning people for his wizardry on Tabla and for being the human that he was.

He learnt his craft from his father, the venerated Ustad Allah Rakha. When he was asked how he would characterize his relationship with his father, he responded that the primary relationship was of a  "Guru" as this role  also subsumed the many dimensions of father, teacher and others. 

As I was reflecting on the above response from Ustad Zakir Hussain, I had an opportunity to watch a dance "Rangapravesha" - formal initiation as a performer with a solo performance of 3 hours.  The performer's mother was her "Guru" - it was a very special moment when she sought her blessings first as her Guru and then as her mother.

Got me thinking. Parents have such a special place in their childrens' hearts. It gets even more special when they play the roles as teachers and Gurus.

Curious to know any such experience you may have had yourselves.

Have a great week ahead 🌤️☀️🙏.

Last week we celebrated the birth anniversary of Shri Atal Bihari Vajpayee ji. He was a true statesman and expressed his disagreements in a graceful manner, while taking his allies and adversaries along.

Last week we lost another respected politician and arguably a statesman Dr. Manmohan Singh ji. He was more of an economist and technocrat and less of a politician. He truly was the father of liberalisation and economic reforms in India - it was unprecedented and perhaps had more naysayers on this *bold* move. These events ground us all and force us to move from hubris to humility. 

These are the real crisis situations that demand decisive, empathetic and agile leadership.  
Many a time, we have the luxury in businesses to discuss and deliberate about the need for such leadership. However in real-life crisis situations which is about protection of lives and mitigating the impact to assets,  there is a need to demonstrate rather than deliberate this brand
of leadership. That surely requires a high level of preparedness and the willingness to lead by example.

Would be curious to hear your thoughts.

The Maha Kumbh Mela commenced the past week and will continue for the next 45 days or so. The Kumbh happens every 12 years and the "Maha" Kumbh happens every 12 Kumbh (effectively144 years). 400 million people from all walks of life, from across the globe are expected to alight at Prayagraj for this mega event. Can you fathom this ocean of humankind?

Organisers of the Kumbh Mela are using AI and installing 300 cameras, including drones, to manage crowds and prevent stampedes at this festival. These are just illustrative - there are other elements of technology that are leveraged as well.

The Maha Kumbh 2025,  promises to be a unique fusion of spirituality and cutting-edge technology.

While it's understandable that Technology is deeply entrenched in all walks of life, spirituality could be one of the surprising frontiers that technology has been able to breach.

10/02/2025, 09:03 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

What caught my attention this week was a post on the LinkedIn by one of my connects, with a link to the Ted Talk by Aravind Srinivas, founder of *Perplexity*, which calls itself an *AI Search engine*.
Interestingly, Aravind also refers to an interview given by Larry Page in 2000, where Larry refers to AI as the ultimate version of Google, synonymous with Search.

Amongst the many points Aravind made,  the point that stood out for me was:

*AI makes a human more human*, since it provides for a higher curiosity and makes us ask more questions. The more questions we ask, the more answers AI provides,  arousing human curiosity and making us ask more. This is a virtuous cycle leading to progress.

This, indeed, is a potential paradigm shift for many of us. Many of us are driven by the need to provide answers, and measure our effectiveness and success by the answers we provide. Now we are somewhat redefining effectiveness and our value, which is determined by our ability to ask powerful questions.

This requires a mindset shift, the seeds of which would need to be sown in the formative stages of life. 

Leadership progression and other associated rewards could be a function of  genuine curiosity and ability to ask powerful questions, more than innate knowledge.

Interesting points to ponder. Curios to hear your perspective of on mindset shift required to leverage and capitalise the power of AI.

Have a curious  week ahead 🌤️☀️🙏.

24/02/2025, 08:27 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

During the week, had an opportunity to listen to some perspectives on engaging the youth. One of the speakers made a couple of powerful points :

1. Engage with the youth - Gen Z, Gen Alpha specifically - by connecting and *communicating in a language they are able to relate to*, and that spurs their interest. They are curious about the "Why" and "WIIFM" (What's In It For Me) and it behoves us to find and provide the answers.

2. Communicate and *engage in a tone and demeanour that does not show any sense of superiority*. He said that every generation believes that the NextGen has diluted their values or not struggled enough to appreciate the value, and almost tend to look down upon them. Its contingent that we start appreciating what the younger generation bring - more enterprising, challenging the status quo, willing to take more risks, etc.

I wonder if we pause to think of the high levels of risks these front-line individuals take to their lives, to deliver comfort to us. 

Many a time, we allude to *"life & death situations"* metaphorically, when we refer to the projects/initiatives that we drive. Let us spare a thought for the front-line human beings in projects such as the two referenced above, who literally face life and death situations on a daily basis, to make life more comfortable for us.
Let us be grateful to them 🙏.

Wonder how can we be more respectful and sensitive to these human beings, and perhaps contribute to their current and future well-being.

24/03/2025, 09:15 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

One of the events that captured the attention of most across the globe was the safe return of astronauts Sunita Williams and Butch Wilmore to the Earth - our home planet - after an unscheduled extended stay in space.
Its truly been a time to celebrate human endurance (at a somewhat abstract level), and more importantly the safe return of two very accomplished astronauts and human beings - Ms. Williams and Mr. Wilmore - at a more identifiable level 💃🕺.

I could not even remotely imagine putting myself into their shoes:
- a trip scheduled for 8 days being extended to 8 months
- being in an alien world, where our simple daily habits and chores which we take for granted have to be significantly changed to lead life that is anything but "normal"
- having to miss the near and dear ones for this period
- knowing well that help will arrive only after many months, with no certainty on exactly when - so training the mind for accepting this
- ... and other dimensions I cannot even fathom...

Many a time we use words like *endurance, resilience*,  and others to advise/suggest the desired disposition to face challenges and bounce back. Sometimes we have struggled to define these words - and these two human beings have brought these words to life, and lent a real meaning by living them.

Similarly *entitlement* is something that seems to creep into our lives- inadvertently or otherwise. Once again, these two have demonstrated that life is about taking it all along the stride, enjoying the journey for what it is, including the goodies that life showers on us, when it seems appropriate - not necessarily when we expect it.

All this requires training our minds and perhaps leading a life with a *Spiritual Quotient* along with IQ, EQ and other elements.

Curious to know how you train your mind.

31/03/2025, 09:05 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week.

The IPL fever is on in the country. We have barely taken off.  Interestingly, in the short voyage so far, we have had an opportunity to get a glimpse of players about whom not much was known till the start of the season. And yet, with just 1-2 matches, they have created an impact. It's indeed exciting, to see new names emerging.

The real test is their long-term sustainability. We  have seen some precocious talent getting unearthed through platforms like IPL. A small percentage go through the grind and come out like a polished diamond, while others fade away.

It's true in all of sports. If we take the contemporary era, we have seen Federer, Nadal, Djokovic and Serena rule the roost in tennis, Messi and Cristiano Ronaldo in soccer,  LeBron James in basketball, Sharath Kamal in table tennis (who retired very recently), Mary Kom in boxing, Vishy Anand in Chess and  many others in other sports as well. We have had exciting challengers challenge these incumbents, but not all of them have been able to transition to NextGen incumbents, save a few.

This phenomenon is not very different in other fields such as movies, businesses and many more. 

This is well characterized by Chandramouli Venkatesan in his book "Catalyst". He talks about looking at a career in two parts.  He adds that about 95% of the people become successful in the first part by any definion of success, while less than 10% become successful in the second part. He exhorts us to not get carried away by short-term success and invest in building stronger foundations - expertise, experience, experimentation, even if uncomfortable -  in the first part, to enhance the probability of success in the second part.

Eager to know your strategy to build your muscle for long-term success.

09/06/2025, 15:42 - Akhil Coach: 𝐒𝐢𝐥𝐞𝐧𝐜𝐞 𝐢𝐧 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐆𝐫𝐨𝐮𝐩 𝐂𝐡𝐚𝐭: 𝐀 𝐒𝐨𝐜𝐢𝐚𝐥 𝐄𝐱𝐩𝐞𝐫𝐢𝐦𝐞𝐧𝐭 𝐨𝐧 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐌𝐨𝐝𝐞𝐫𝐧 𝐇𝐢𝐧𝐝𝐮 𝐌𝐢𝐧𝐝𝐬𝐞𝐭

What began as a casual social experiment during a time of national tension soon revealed something far more profound. Arpan, an old schoolmate, and I — both alumni of one of Ranchi’s most prestigious schools, Class of 1988, 89 and 90 — decided to observe how our batchmates would respond to real-world issues. This wasn’t just any alumni group of 600+ members. It included some of the brightest minds from our generation: one-third settled abroad in the U.S. or Europe, the rest in India — primarily in high-paying IT jobs, as doctors, bureaucrats, and senior management professionals. Even the backbenchers — once dismissed — had gone on to become successful entrepreneurs and politicians.

Naturally, we assumed this was a group rich in perspective, curiosity, and the intellectual courage to engage in meaningful discourse. We were mistaken.

When the Pahalgam incident occurred, we brought it up for discussion. The first response? “No heavy topics, please.” The group preferred light-hearted memes, birthday greetings, and nostalgic throwbacks. Even as tensions escalated into armed conflict, nothing changed. When we tried to raise issues such as radicalisation or the root causes of terrorism, we were privately cautioned not to “offend anyone,” because “Muslims are also part of this group.” Even as drones hovered and ballistic missiles were fired, the chat remained eerily silent.

We shared messages like “Jai Shree Ram” and “Jai Hind” — especially following India’s military achievements. Still, not a single word of solidarity. No acknowledgment. No pride. When we criticized Pakistan’s provocations, a few responded — not with concern, but with deflections: “What about caste discrimination in India?” A textbook case of false equivalence. That’s when the realization struck: this wasn’t mere indifference. We were witnessing a collective psychological shift among modern, educated Hindus — a shift with far-reaching implications.

⸻

The Psychology Behind the Silence

1. Post-Colonial Hangover
We are products of an education system that often celebrated Western ideals while subtly — or overtly — undermining Indian civilizational thought. Colonial narratives portrayed Indian culture as regressive and hierarchical. Even post-independence, we internalized these narratives. As a result, many Hindus today instinctively distance themselves from their own heritage in order to appear “modern,” “neutral,” or “progressive.”

2. Guilt Conditioning by Academia and Media
From textbooks to television, modern Indians are taught to view Hinduism through a lens of guilt — emphasizing caste, patriarchy, and superstition, while ignoring its profound contributions to philosophy, art, science, and spirituality. This one-sided narrative conditions many to feel discomfort — even shame — when expressing anything remotely Hindu in public, including something as simple as saying “Jai Shree Ram.”

3. Global Citizen Syndrome
Many who live abroad or work in global corporations adopt the identity of a “global citizen.” In this worldview, national pride or cultural rootedness is often seen as parochial or even dangerous. Cultural ambiguity and political correctness become aspirational. On platforms like WhatsApp, where social and professional reputations are at stake, nationalist expression is viewed as a liability.

4. Fear of Social Isolation
In elite circles, being perceived as “too Hindu” or “right-wing” can invite mockery, labeling, or exclusion. Consequently, even those who privately agree with certain views remain publicly silent. Memes are safe. Birthday wishes are safe. “Jai Hind” is not. This fear fuels an echo chamber where difficult truths are quietly buried.

5. Extreme Individualism
Economic progress has brought with it a hyper-focus on personal success: careers, EMIs, foreign vacations, and nuclear families. Cultural responsibility, civic engagement, and national identity are seen as unnecessary extras. The dominant mindset becomes: “I pay taxes and vote — that’s enough.”

6. Selective Moral Outrage
Strikingly, many of these same individuals are quick to speak out — but only when the cause aligns with global liberal narratives: climate change, BLM, Gaza, Ukraine, etc. But when Hindus face violence, or when India is under threat, those same voices fall conspicuously silent. Whether due to fear, confusion, or indifference — the impact is the same: absence.

⸻

The Larger Implication

This silence is not incidental — it is symptomatic of a deeper civilizational crisis. When even the most educated, well-positioned Hindus feel hesitant or embarrassed to express cultural pride or national solidarity, we are witnessing a profound disconnection from our roots.

The tragedy isn’t that adversaries exist. The tragedy is that the sentinels — the very people who should be standing guard — are asleep.

Our social experiment didn’t reveal a loud collapse. It revealed a quiet erosion. Not violent, but telling. And if we do not recognize, confront, and reverse this decline, the silence we’re hearing today may well become the soundtrack of a fading civilization.

As I pause and look back on the path of my life,
I realize—there has always been a guiding presence.
Someone who walked beside me through every storm,
Who lit a lamp when darkness clouded my way,
Who offered silence when words would only confuse.

The Guru—not just a person,
But the flame that dispels ignorance,
The mirror that reveals who I truly am,
The silent force that uplifts, awakens, and anchors.

At times, a mother in compassion.
At times, a father in strength.
At times, the stillness that led me inward.

Guru Purnima is not merely a celebration—
It is a sacred remembrance of a bond beyond time—
Where the disciple surrenders not just their mind,
But their entire being at the feet of the Guru.

Today, with folded hands and a humbled heart, I say—
Gurudev, what you have given me
cannot be measured or repaid.
May the fire of devotion you lit within me
burn ever bright, ever steady.

🙏 Countless salutations to you, O beacon of truth and grace. 🙏

11/08/2025, 09:00 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you all had a good week .

*Trust & Transparency* - they have an interesting relationship. Transparency leads to deepening trust, yet a certain level of trust is required between parties to feel comfortable to be transparent. 

During the week, in two separate unrelated instances, there was lack of transparency from one party. In both cases, these individuals gave excuses to not show up, with very short notice. In both cases, the other party saw thru the excuse, as this has happened before as well. They felt that the originators of excuse could have been transparent, thereby building trust, instead of destroying it. Being transparent requires the person to be vulnerable, which requires courage. In one case, it was a peer, and in another case, it was a team member, thereby demonstrating that "being transparent" is not the exclusive domain of the party at a higher level in an asymmetric relationship like manager-team member. If any, transparency makes it a relationship of equals.

Equally, there are individuals, in-spite of their positions, share information, especially when things are not going well, and demonstrate  vulnerability. I have been fortunate to experience it in close quarters. Needless to say, in those cases, the official "positions" have blurred and the relationships deepend.

Eager to hear your experiences.

Have a wonderful week ahead 🌤️☀️🙏.

13/10/2025, 06:57 - Anant Krishnan: Very good morning. Hope you had a good week.

Over the weekend, I had an opportunity to attend the "Rangapravesha" of one of my daughter's friends. This event is essentially the formal initiation of a performer (dancer or musician)  and a sort of graduation from an amateur to a professional, through a solo performance for about 3 hours. It doesn't come easy.

The performer goes through an extensive learning journey - typically over a decade and more - with a "Guru",  before the Guru ascertains the readiness of the "Shishya" (disciple) for this performance. After the Shishya is deemed ready, there is a lot of sweat that goes into the preparation for this special event, not just by the performer, (Shishya), but equally  and more by her Guru. 

This journey of a decade and more, where the Guru accompanies the Shishya on her journey, and creates a unique path for her, is very special. The Guru is a mentor, coach and much more in this life journey. Today this is perhaps confined to just the fields of music and dance.

Sports, comes closer in terms of a similar relationship between the ward and the coach , though the tenure could be a lot shorter. 

In the corporate and business circles, there are some illustrious individuals who play the role of a coach and/or mentor and beyond, and organically earn the place of a "Guru".
Only the fortunate few have the benefit of such "gurus" in their journeys.

Curious to know about your experiences having had such "invested mentors" in your life. 
You may also be that "guru" for someone else. Your mentees would be better positioned to acknowledge your impact on their lives.
